========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, thanks. I guess what really pushed me to come in is that people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him, I swear I’m trying to pay attention, and then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. Then I have to ask him to repeat it, and it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m being careless.

And it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll nod along, but then I’ll realize I missed pieces of it, and I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say?” or “Can you say that again?” It’s frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m choosing not to listen. It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. That’s been causing enough problems that I figured I should talk to someone about it.

I think so? I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. It’s more like… when people are talking, especially if there’s a lot going on or it’s a longer explanation, I hear that they’re talking but I don’t always hang onto all the words.

But no, I haven’t noticed anything like needing the TV super loud or people sounding muffled all the time. At least not that I’m aware of. I guess I’ve never had it formally checked recently or anything, so I can’t say for sure, but it doesn’t feel to me like my ears aren’t working.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what really pushed me to come in is that people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him, I swear I’m trying to pay attention, and then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. Then I have to ask him to repeat it, and it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m being careless.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll nod along, but then I’ll realize I missed pieces of it, and I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say?” or “Can you say that again?” It’s frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m choosing not to listen. It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. It’s more like… when people are talking, especially if there’s a lot going on or it’s a longer explanation, I hear that they’re talking but I don’t always hang onto all the words.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like needing the TV super loud or people sounding muffled all the time. Yeah, I think that’s probably what’s happening a lot of the time. Like, I don’t always notice the exact moment my mind goes somewhere else, but I’ll be standing there with my supervisor talking right to me, and I’m looking at him, nodding, trying to follow, and then suddenly I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. It’s embarrassing because then I have to ask him to go back over it, and it looks like I wasn’t taking it seriously.

Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him, I swear I’m trying to pay attention, and then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. Then I have to ask him to repeat it, and it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m being careless.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll nod along, but then I’ll realize I missed pieces of it, and I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say?” or “Can you say that again?” It’s frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m choosing not to listen. It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. That’s been causing enough problems that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hearing is normal?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. It’s more like… when people are talking, especially if there’s a lot going on or it’s a longer explanation, I hear that they’re talking but I don’t always hang onto all the words.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like needing the TV super loud or people sounding muffled all the time. At least not that I’m aware of. I guess I’ve never had it formally checked recently or anything, so I can’t say for sure, but it doesn’t feel to me like my ears aren’t working.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, does your mind often drift to something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably what’s happening a lot of the time. Like, I don’t always notice the exact moment my mind goes somewhere else, but I’ll be standing there with my supervisor talking right to me, and I’m looking at him, nodding, trying to follow, and then suddenly I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. It’s embarrassing because then I have to ask him to go back over it, and it looks like I wasn’t taking it seriously.\n\nSame thing with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll think I’m listening, but then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was that part?” or “Can you say that again?” So yeah, I guess my mind must be drifting or not holding onto it, even though I’m not meaning to tune people out.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah. Honestly, looking back, I think I was like this even as a kid, just in a more obvious way maybe. I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I couldn’t stay put or I’d be bothering the kids near me. Like they’d put me closer to the front, or away from certain people, because I was always turning around or getting into something.

And I’d call out answers before I was supposed to. I remember getting in trouble for that—like the teacher would ask the class something and I’d just blurt it out, even if I hadn’t been called on. It wasn’t like I was trying to be rude, I just… I don’t know, I’d hear the question and the answer would jump out of my mouth before I really thought about waiting.

I also used to butt into other kids’ work, like if someone was doing an assignment next to me, I’d lean over and tell them what to do or start messing with their paper or talk to them when I wasn’t supposed to. That was before middle school, for sure. I got corrected for it a lot. So yeah, I’d say this kind of thing has been there a long time.

Yeah. Very much so. Sitting still is… I don’t know, it’s not just boredom, exactly. It feels physical, like there’s this pressure that starts building up inside me, kind of in my chest and down in my legs, like my body is telling me I need to get up or move or do something right now.

If I have to just sit there, it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like I’m trying to hold myself in place, but everything inside is pushing the other way. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay still, just sit like a normal person,” but it’s almost uncomfortable, like an itch you can’t scratch unless you move. It’s hard to explain, but it’s not calm. My body just feels revved up.

Like I’m trying to hold myself in place, but everything inside is pushing the other way. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay still, just sit like a normal person,” but it’s almost uncomfortable, like an itch you can’t scratch unless you move. It’s hard to explain, but it’s not calm. My body just feels revved up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these attention and restlessness problems caused significant problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work it has gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings, and I worry I could lose my job or cause a safety problem on the line. At home, my wife and kids get frustrated because I disrupt routines instead of helping them run smoothly.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying or something I can work around, but it’s caused real problems.

At work, it’s gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings. Not just someone saying, “Hey, pay attention,” but actual warnings where I’m sitting there thinking, okay, this is in my file, this could turn into me losing my job. And on the production line, that scares me because it’s not only about me being slower or someone getting irritated with me. If I miss something or I’m restless and moving around when I shouldn’t be, it could create a safety problem. That part really bothers me. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?

And at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.

So yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.

Then I have to ask him to repeat it, and it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m being careless.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll nod along, but then I’ll realize I missed pieces of it, and I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say?” or “Can you say that again?” It’s frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m choosing not to listen. It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. It wasn’t like I was trying to be rude, I just… I don’t know, I’d hear the question and the answer would jump out of my mouth before I really thought about waiting.\n\nI also used to butt into other kids’ work, like if someone was doing an assignment next to me, I’d lean over and tell them what to do or start messing with their paper or talk to them when I wasn’t supposed to. That was before middle school, for sure. I got corrected for it a lot. So yeah, I’d say this kind of thing has been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. Sitting still is… I don’t know, it’s not just boredom, exactly. It feels physical, like there’s this pressure that starts building up inside me, kind of in my chest and down in my legs, like my body is telling me I need to get up or move or do something right now.\n\nIf I have to just sit there, it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like I’m trying to hold myself in place, but everything inside is pushing the other way. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay still, just sit like a normal person,” but it’s almost uncomfortable, like an itch you can’t scratch unless you move. It’s hard to explain, but it’s not calm. My body just feels revved up.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused significant problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying or something I can work around, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings. Not just someone saying, “Hey, pay attention,” but actual warnings where I’m sitting there thinking, okay, this is in my file, this could turn into me losing my job. And on the production line, that scares me because it’s not only about me being slower or someone getting irritated with me. If I miss something or I’m restless and moving around when I shouldn’t be, it could create a safety problem. That part really bothers me. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.\n\nSo yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo people often sound muffled when they talk to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Muffled? Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense that people sound like they’re underwater or like I can’t hear them clearly. I haven’t really noticed that as a thing.

I mean, sometimes I might miss what somebody said and have to ask them to repeat it, but I wouldn’t describe it as their voice sounding muffled exactly. It’s more just… I don’t know, I didn’t catch it in the moment. But no, I don’t walk around feeling like people’s voices are blocked or unclear like that.

It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. It’s not only when I’m stressed out or upset or something. Even on a normal day, when I feel pretty calm, I still catch myself not really staying with what I’m supposed to be doing.

Like on the line, there are quality checks I’m supposed to slow down and do carefully, and I’ll tell myself, okay, look at the label, check the fitting, make sure it’s right. But then I rush through it anyway, almost like my brain is already trying to get to the next thing, and I miss small stuff. A wrong label, or a loose fitting, things that I should have caught if I was really locked in.

And then my supervisor has to pull me aside, which is embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the checks. I do know. That’s the frustrating part. I can be calm, I can understand the task, and I can still miss those little details because I’m not holding my attention on it long enough. And it keeps happening, so it’s not just one bad day.

I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying or something I can work around, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings. And on the production line, that scares me because it’s not only about me being slower or someone getting irritated with me. If I miss something or I’m restless and moving around when I shouldn’t be, it could create a safety problem. That part really bothers me. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.\n\nSo yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do people often sound muffled when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: Muffled? Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense that people sound like they’re underwater or like I can’t hear them clearly. I haven’t really noticed that as a thing.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might miss what somebody said and have to ask them to repeat it, but I wouldn’t describe it as their voice sounding muffled exactly. It’s more just… I don’t know, I didn’t catch it in the moment. But no, I don’t walk around feeling like people’s voices are blocked or unclear like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you still have trouble paying attention even when you feel calm and not stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not only when I’m stressed out or upset or something. Even on a normal day, when I feel pretty calm, I still catch myself not really staying with what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike on the line, there are quality checks I’m supposed to slow down and do carefully, and I’ll tell myself, okay, look at the label, check the fitting, make sure it’s right. But then I rush through it anyway, almost like my brain is already trying to get to the next thing, and I miss small stuff. A wrong label, or a loose fitting, things that I should have caught if I was really locked in.\n\nAnd then my supervisor has to pull me aside, which is embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the checks. I do know. That’s the frustrating part. I can be calm, I can understand the task, and I can still miss those little details because I’m not holding my attention on it long enough. And it keeps happening, so it’s not just one bad day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 9\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say depressed exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and there are days where I feel worn down by everything, but “down or depressed” feels like a different thing, maybe.

I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed lately. It’s more like I get aggravated or embarrassed when things don’t go well, especially when I feel like I should be able to handle something and then I don’t. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time or anything like that.

So… I guess my answer is no, not really depressed. More frustrated than depressed, if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.2, H91.90, R41.840, F41.9, F32.A, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what really pushed me to come in is that people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him, I swear I’m trying to pay attention, and then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. Then I have to ask him to repeat it, and it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m being careless.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll nod along, but then I’ll realize I missed pieces of it, and I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say?” or “Can you say that again?” It’s frustrating because I don’t feel like I’m choosing not to listen. It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. That’s been causing enough problems that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hearing is normal?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. It’s more like… when people are talking, especially if there’s a lot going on or it’s a longer explanation, I hear that they’re talking but I don’t always hang onto all the words.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like needing the TV super loud or people sounding muffled all the time. At least not that I’m aware of. I guess I’ve never had it formally checked recently or anything, so I can’t say for sure, but it doesn’t feel to me like my ears aren’t working.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, does your mind often drift to something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably what’s happening a lot of the time. Like, I don’t always notice the exact moment my mind goes somewhere else, but I’ll be standing there with my supervisor talking right to me, and I’m looking at him, nodding, trying to follow, and then suddenly I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. It’s embarrassing because then I have to ask him to go back over it, and it looks like I wasn’t taking it seriously.\n\nSame thing with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll think I’m listening, but then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was that part?” or “Can you say that again?” So yeah, I guess my mind must be drifting or not holding onto it, even though I’m not meaning to tune people out.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Honestly, looking back, I think I was like this even as a kid, just in a more obvious way maybe. I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I couldn’t stay put or I’d be bothering the kids near me. Like they’d put me closer to the front, or away from certain people, because I was always turning around or getting into something.\n\nAnd I’d call out answers before I was supposed to. I remember getting in trouble for that—like the teacher would ask the class something and I’d just blurt it out, even if I hadn’t been called on. It wasn’t like I was trying to be rude, I just… I don’t know, I’d hear the question and the answer would jump out of my mouth before I really thought about waiting.\n\nI also used to butt into other kids’ work, like if someone was doing an assignment next to me, I’d lean over and tell them what to do or start messing with their paper or talk to them when I wasn’t supposed to. That was before middle school, for sure. I got corrected for it a lot. So yeah, I’d say this kind of thing has been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. Sitting still is… I don’t know, it’s not just boredom, exactly. It feels physical, like there’s this pressure that starts building up inside me, kind of in my chest and down in my legs, like my body is telling me I need to get up or move or do something right now.\n\nIf I have to just sit there, it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like I’m trying to hold myself in place, but everything inside is pushing the other way. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Just stay still, just sit like a normal person,” but it’s almost uncomfortable, like an itch you can’t scratch unless you move. It’s hard to explain, but it’s not calm. My body just feels revved up.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused significant problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s just annoying or something I can work around, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings. Not just someone saying, “Hey, pay attention,” but actual warnings where I’m sitting there thinking, okay, this is in my file, this could turn into me losing my job. And on the production line, that scares me because it’s not only about me being slower or someone getting irritated with me. If I miss something or I’m restless and moving around when I shouldn’t be, it could create a safety problem. That part really bothers me. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.\n\nSo yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do people often sound muffled when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: Muffled? Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense that people sound like they’re underwater or like I can’t hear them clearly. I haven’t really noticed that as a thing.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might miss what somebody said and have to ask them to repeat it, but I wouldn’t describe it as their voice sounding muffled exactly. It’s more just… I don’t know, I didn’t catch it in the moment. But no, I don’t walk around feeling like people’s voices are blocked or unclear like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you still have trouble paying attention even when you feel calm and not stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not only when I’m stressed out or upset or something. Even on a normal day, when I feel pretty calm, I still catch myself not really staying with what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike on the line, there are quality checks I’m supposed to slow down and do carefully, and I’ll tell myself, okay, look at the label, check the fitting, make sure it’s right. And it keeps happening, so it’s not just one bad day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say depressed exactly. I’m not sure I’d call it worrying a lot, no. I mean, I think about things, sure, like anybody does, and if something’s actually going on then I can get caught up in it for a bit. But when there isn’t an immediate problem, I don’t think I’m just sitting there worrying all the time for no reason.

Sometimes my mind is busy, if that’s what you mean, but it doesn’t always feel like worry. It’s more like thoughts jumping around or me thinking about what I need to do next. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly anxious about things that haven’t happened or anything like that.

So… no, I don’t think that’s a big thing for me. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.

It’s more like the words are coming in but not all of them stick, if that makes sense. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it as a hearing problem. Like, I can hear sounds fine as far as I know—if someone drops something or calls my name, I usually hear it. I guess I’ve never had it formally checked recently or anything, so I can’t say for sure, but it doesn’t feel to me like my ears aren’t working.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, does your mind often drift to something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably what’s happening a lot of the time. Like, I don’t always notice the exact moment my mind goes somewhere else, but I’ll be standing there with my supervisor talking right to me, and I’m looking at him, nodding, trying to follow, and then suddenly I realize I only caught maybe half of what he said. It’s embarrassing because then I have to ask him to go back over it, and it looks like I wasn’t taking it seriously.\n\nSame thing with my wife. She’ll be talking straight to me, and I’ll think I’m listening, but then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was that part?” or “Can you say that again?” So yeah, I guess my mind must be drifting or not holding onto it, even though I’m not meaning to tune people out.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Honestly, looking back, I think I was like this even as a kid, just in a more obvious way maybe. That was before middle school, for sure. I got corrected for it a lot. Sitting still is… I don’t know, it’s not just boredom, exactly. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.\n\nSo yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do people often sound muffled when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: Muffled? Um… I don’t think so. If I miss something or I’m restless and moving around when I shouldn’t be, it could create a safety problem. That part really bothers me. I’ll go home thinking about it, like, what if next time it’s not just a warning?\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects things there too. My wife gets frustrated, and honestly my kids do too. There are routines in the house—getting ready, meals, bedtime, just normal family stuff—and instead of helping things go smoothly, I can end up disrupting it. Like I’m jumping in, interrupting the flow, not sticking with what we’re supposed to be doing, and then everyone else has to deal with the chaos I’m adding. My wife will be trying to keep things on track, and I can tell she feels like I’m making it harder, not easier.\n\nSo yes, it’s significant. It’s not just in my head or just a personality thing. It’s affecting my job and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do people often sound muffled when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: Muffled? Um… I don’t think so. Yeah, it’s definitely more than one place. Work is where it gets noticed because there are consequences, you know, my supervisor is right there and if I miss something or don’t catch what he said, it shows up pretty quick.

But it happens at home too. Honestly, what brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"additional_requirement_1\",\"name\":\"Additional Requirement 1\",\"description\":\"Onset prior to age 12 years\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether these attention-related difficulties were present during childhood, which is directly about symptom onset before age 12.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.   }
